Paionian

Appears on every character, agents included · sons inherit it 1% of the time · never on 12 of the cultures. Once it reaches Paionian it cannot drop back.

Effects: no stat effects · grants the epithet the Paionian

This man is a Paionian, a descendent of the legendary Paion. They live in the Axios river basin as far as the Strymon, situated between the Macedonians, Illyrians, Dardanians, and Thracians. After their subjugation by Philip II, they've been known and recruited for their skills as cavalrymen, an ability that allows them to defend themselves against their many dangerous neighbours.

Cultures that never receive it (12)

Celtiberian · Brittonic · Roman · Egyptian · Ethiopian · Phoenician · Libyan · Caucasian · Anatolian · Iranian · Arab · Indian

Granted by events, at birth or by inheritance.

Parian

Appears on every character, agents included · sons inherit it 1% of the time · never on 17 of the cultures. Once it reaches Parian it cannot drop back.

Effects: no stat effects · grants the epithet of Paros

This man is from Paros in the Cyclades, once settled by a guy called Paros with Arcadians and famed for its marble. The homonymous Polis sided with the Persians during the Persian Wars and it was where Miltiades the Younger of Athens received the wound that would kill him.

Parthinoi

Appears on every character, agents included · sons inherit it 1% of the time · never on 12 of the cultures. Once it reaches Parthinoi it cannot drop back.

Effects: no stat effects · grants the epithet the Parthinian

This man hails from the Parthinoi, a major Illyrian tribe located between the Greek cities of Apollonia and Epidamnos to the west, the Taulantioi to the north and the Dassaretoi to the east. With their capital in Dimale, they have settled in a powerful position, controlling the area between the Dassaretis, the access point to Macedonia, and the Adriatic Sea.

Cultures that never receive it (12)

Celtiberian · Brittonic · Roman · Egyptian · Ethiopian · Phoenician · Libyan · Caucasian · Anatolian · Iranian · Arab · Indian

Granted by events, at birth or by inheritance.

Passed Under the Yoke

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders.

Effects: -3 Influence, -1 Command, +2 Unrest

This general’s army has suffered the ultimate humiliation, an abhorrence to Roman dignitas. He has surrendered his army to the enemy, and been made to pass under the oxen’s yoke made by the enemy’s spears. The whole of the City is in an uproar over this ultimate of humiliations, and the plebs are demanding blood in vengeance. This loss will taint his family for generations to come.\n\n Very early Roman or Etruscan armies would have their defeated enemies stripped of their arms and armor and forced them to march beneath a yoke made of crossed spears. This forced the defeated men to kneel as they passed beneath it, as if to a king, which was the ultimate humiliation.\n\n Unfortunately, Rome's enemies adopted the idea as well, with the result that from time to time a Roman army was forced to pass beneath the yoke. To do this was an intolerable humiliation, so much so that the Senate would have preferred an army to fight to the last man than suffer such a fate.

Pentarch

Effects: +1 Influence

The Pentarchs are five magistrates who hold great power, and might the real power in Carthage. \nThe magistrates of five have under them many important matters: choosing the supreme council of One Hundred and Four (who answer to these men), and should hold office longer than other magistrates (for they are virtually rulers both before and after they hold office). These are oligarchical features, but their being without salary and not elected by lot are characteristic of aristocracy, thus the two forms of government are mixed together.

Perioikos

Appears on every character, agents included · pulls against Spartiates, Neodamodeis · never on 17 of the cultures.

Effects: +1 Management, +5 Trading, -1 Influence

Under the rule of Sparta, the perioikoi ‘dwellers round about’, were neighbouring people, often constituting groups of subjects or half-citizens, normally with local self-government. The best known group of perioikoi are those of the Spartan state. The origins of their status and ethnic affiliation are unclear, though their dialect was the Laconian Doric common to all the state's inhabitants.\nLike the full citizen Spartiates, perioikoi were counted as Lacedaemonians in military contexts, serving not only in the Spartan army but even in the same regiments. But they had no say in the making of Spartan policy and seem to have been subject to special taxation, and so can be considered at best second-class citizens of Sparta. Their status in Sparta was akin to that of an ally in the Peloponnesian League. The perioikic poleis possessed local autonomy and their own religious sanctuaries but were entirely subject to Sparta in foreign policy. Social stratification within the perioikic communities is on record; we read in Xenophon both of ‘gentlemen’ perioikoi and of a named perioikic cavalryman; they were presumably substantial landowners. But humbler perioikoi profited from the Spartans' abstention from all economic activity, by providing them with raw materials (notably iron) and objects of manufacture and trade. Gytheum, the most important community, served Sparta both as chief port and as naval dockyard and muster station.\n\nThis man would make a good Governor, as he can acquire the economic traits and professions that Spartiates cannot, and he can be used as a General if necessary.

Cultures that never receive it (17)

Gallic · Germanic · Dacian · Celtiberian · Brittonic · Thracian · Scythian · Roman · Egyptian · Ethiopian · Phoenician · Libyan · Caucasian · Anatolian · Iranian · Arab · Indian

How it is gained — at the end of the character's turn
WhenPointsChanceConditions
at the end of the character's turn+1100%belongs to Sparta · is a family member · ended the turn in a settlement · in Sparta · does not have Spartiates

Physkon

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · at most 10 characters may hold it · never on 17 of the cultures.

Effects: -1 Influence · grants the epithet Physkon

This man has been cursed with a figure that inspires both ridicule and affection, earning him the nickname 'the Fatty'. While his size has made him a frequent target of jesters and poets, it has also earned him an air of approachable charm. He may be slow on the battlefield, but his appetite for feasts and life itself is unmatched. Unfortunately, his indulgence leaves little room for ambition, and his political influence shrinks as his belt expands.

Plain

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 5 levels · pulls against Good Looking. Once it reaches Plain it cannot drop back.

Plain1-1 InfluenceHomely2-2 InfluenceUgly3-3 InfluenceRepellent4-4 Influence
Scares Small Children · epithet the Ugly5-5 Influence
What the game says at each level

Plain — This man's appearance, though unremarkable, doesn't interfere with his ambitions, he’s well aware that there are more important things than looks. After all, not everyone can be a statue-worthy hero, and he’s perfectly fine with that. At least his personality doesn’t frighten the horses.

Homely — Some call him ugly, but he insists he's just 'ruggedly handsome.' Unfortunately, it seems the world is blind to his unique charm, leaving him to win hearts through sheer persistence, or at least silence his critics with a glare.

Ugly — This man would like to think of himself as "rugged", but "as ugly as the north end of a pig going south" would be closer to the truth. While he tries his best to compensate with wit and bravery, the odds are not in his favor.

Repellent — His face is a force of nature, and not in a good way. Even a well-timed joke or persuading speech can’t distract from the unfortunate truth, that his appearance, while unique, is not one that endears him to others, no matter how kind his heart or how sharp his mind is.

Scares Small Children — When he enters a room, children flee and grown men avoid his eyes. There’s ugly, and then there’s this, an appearance so shocking it makes young children cry at the mere sight of him, and even the bravest of adults struggle to maintain eye contact. Some say even statues tremble in his presence.

Plebeian

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Equestrian, Patrician · never on 21 of the cultures.

Effects: -1 Influence

This man comes from the plebeian class, the lowest class of Roman citizens. With the passing of the Lex Hortensia in 287 BC, the plebeians gained formal political equality with the patricians, but this "equality" is often more a legal fiction than a lived reality, as the Consilium Plebis is headed by the Tribunes of the Plebs who are usually close to those of senatorial class. In the majority of the time, the patricians and those who are influential work together in the Senate to maintain their grip on power.

Pogon

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · at most 3 characters may hold it · never on 17 of the cultures.

Effects: no stat effects · grants the epithet Pogon

This philosopher, with a beard that could shelter a small family of sparrows, is the embodiment of ancient wisdom. His whiskers flow as freely as his thoughts, and both are subjects of great admiration. While others rely on scrolls to make their arguments, he merely strokes his beard and imparts wisdom so profound that listeners are left awestruck. Some say his beard is a source of divine inspiration.

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · at most 2 characters may hold it · never on 17 of the cultures.

Effects: +2 Siege Attack · grants the epithet Poliorketes

This man is so good at taking cities from their former owners that he has been hailed with the title Poliorketes, meaning 'good besieger'. Whether through relentless siege engines, cunning stratagems, or sheer persistence, his name strikes fear into those behind city walls. While grateful cities may bestow the title in admiration, his enemies probably mutter it with fear while preparing their defences.

Popularis Sympathiser

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 4 levels · pulls against Optimate Sympathiser · never on 21 of the cultures. Once it reaches Staunch Popularis it cannot drop back.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Popularis Sympathiser2no stat effectsPopularis5no stat effectsStaunch Popularis9+1 Local Popularity, -1 InfluenceDiehard Popularis14+2 Local Popularity, -2 Influence
What the game says at each level

Popularis Sympathiser — This man has begun to take a quiet interest in the hardships of ordinary Romans such as the price of grain, the landless veterans, the grievances of the poor. He has not yet acted on these sympathies in any meaningful way, but he no longer looks away from them either, and his friends among the old families have started to notice a certain restlessness in him.

Popularis — This man now belongs to the Popularis, who "favour the people", the popular assemblies, as opposed to the Optimates and the Senate. He thinks those governed should have more say in their governance, especially if that means weakening the power of the Nobiles.

Staunch Popularis — This man is now fully committed to the cause of the Popularis, who "favour the people", the popular assemblies, as opposed to the Optimates and the Senate. He thinks those governed should have more say in their governance, and speaks of the dignity of the common citizen as a cause worth risking his career, and perhaps his life, to defend.

Diehard Popularis — This man has concluded that the Republic's institutions will never grant the people what they are owed, and that half-measures and patient petitioning have failed. He is prepared to break with precedent, defy the Senate outright, and risk everything, his office, his standing, his life, for the cause he has made his own. Men like him are remembered either as liberators or as the men who tore the Republic apart, and history has not yet decided which he will be.

Praefectus Aegypto

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Praefectus Aegypto1no stat effectsPraefectus Aegypto2+2 InfluencePraefectus Aegypto3+2 Influence, +1 Farming, +2 Trading
What the game says at each level

Praefectus Aegypto — This man is the Prefect of Egypt, established as a province in 30 BC, after Octavian defeated his rival Mark Antony, deposed Queen Cleopatra VII, and annexed the Ptolemaic kingdom of Egypt. The province encompasses most of modern-day Egypt except for the Sinai Peninsula (which would later be conquered by Trajan). Egypt would come to serve as a major producer of grain for the empire. As a province it was ruled by a prefect instead of the traditional senatorial governor of other Roman provinces. The prefect was a man of equestrian rank and was appointed by the Emperor. From the reign of Nero onward Egypt enjoyed an era of prosperity which lasted a century. Much trouble was caused by religious conflicts between the Greeks and the Jews, particularly in Alexandria, which after the destruction of Jerusalem in 70 AD became the world centre of Jewish religion and culture. \nThe effect of the Roman conquest was at first to strengthen the position of the Greeks and of Hellenism against Egyptian influences. The Romans introduced important changes in the administrative system, aimed at achieving a high level of efficiency and maximizing revenue. The duties of the prefect of Egypt combined responsibility for military security through command of the legions and cohorts, for the organization of finance and taxation, and for the administration of justice.

Praefectus Augusti Iudaeae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Praefectus Augusti Iudaeae1no stat effectsPraefectus Augusti Iudaeae2+1 Influence, +40 Bribe ResistancePraefectus Augusti Iudaeae3+1 Influence, +4 Unrest, -2 Law, +40 Bribe Resistance, +10 Tax Collection
What the game says at each level

Praefectus Augusti Iudaeae — This man is the Imperial Prefect of Judea, which isn't a senatorial province, nor an imperial province, but instead a "satellite of Syria", governed by a prefect who is a knight of the Equestrian Order.\nIn in 4 CE, a messianic revolt erupted in Judea because of Archelaus's incompetence; the revolt was brutally crushed by the Legate of Syria, Publius Quinctilius Varus, who occupied Jerusalem and crucified 2000 Jewish rebels. Because of his failure to properly rule Judea, in 6 CE Archelaus was removed from his post by Augustus and Judea, Samaria and Idumea came under direct Roman administration; the Roman governor was given authority to punish by execution, while the general population also began to be taxed by Rome.\nJudea’s revenue was of little importance to the Roman treasury, but it controlled the land and coastal sea routes to the "bread basket" of Egypt and was a buffer against the Parthian Empire. The capital was also moved from Jerusalem to Caesarea Maritima.\nBetween 41 and 44 CE, Judea regained its nominal autonomy when Herod Agrippa was made “King of the Jews” by the emperor Claudius. Following Agrippa's death in 44, the province returned to direct Roman control, incorporating Agrippa's personal territories of Galilee and Peraea, under a row of procurators, but from 70 until 135 Judea's rebelliousness required a governing Roman legate capable of commanding legions.

Praefectus Augusti Iudaeae — This man is the Imperial Prefect of Judea, which isn't a senatorial province, nor an imperial province, but instead a "satellite of Syria", governed by a prefect who is a knight of the Equestrian Order.\nIn in 4 CE, a messianic revolt erupted in Judea because of Archelaus's incompetence; the revolt was brutally crushed by the Legate of Syria, Publius Quinctilius Varus, who occupied Jerusalem and crucified 2000 Jewish rebels. Because of his failure to properly rule Judea, in 6 CE Archelaus was removed from his post by Augustus and Judea, Samaria and Idumea came under direct Roman administration; the Roman governor was given authority to punish by execution, while the general population also began to be taxed by Rome.\nJudea’s revenue was of little importance to the Roman treasury, but it controlled the land and coastal sea routes to the "bread basket" of Egypt and was a buffer against the Parthian Empire. The capital was also moved from Jerusalem to Caesarea Maritima.\nBetween 41 and 44 CE, Judea regained its nominal autonomy when Herod Agrippa was made “King of the Jews” by the emperor Claudius. Following Agrippa's death in 44, the province returned to direct Roman control, incorporating Agrippa's personal territories of Galilee and Peraea, under a row of procurators, but from 70 until 135 Judea's rebelliousness required a governing Roman legate capable of commanding legions.

What the game says at each level

Praefectus Augusti Mesopotamiae — This man is the Imperial Prefect of Mesopotamia.\nIn 113 Emperor Trajan (r. 98-117) launched a war against Rome's long-time eastern rival, the Parthian Empire. In 114, he conquered Armenia, which was made into a province, and by the end of 115 he had conquered northern Mesopotamia. This too was organized as a province in early 116 when coins were minted to celebrate the fact.\nLater in the same year, Trajan marched into central and southern Mesopotamia (enlarging and completing the province of Mesopotamia) and across the river Tigris to Adiabene, which he annexed into another Roman province Assyria.\n\nBut he did not stop there. In the last months of 116, he captured the great Persian city of Susa, deposed the Parthian king Osroes I and put his own puppet ruler Parthamaspates on the Parthian throne. Never again would the Roman Empire advance so far to the east.\nAs soon as Trajan died, his successor Hadrian (r. 117-138) relinquished his conquests east of the Euphrates river, which again became the Roman Empire's eastern boundary.\n\nNow your turn has come. Will you try and keep the region under your control, or will this be too much of a burden for your Empire?

Praefectus Castrorum

Effects: +1 Law, +1 Troop Morale, +5 Bribe Resistance

Usually an ex-Primus Pilus, this man has risen through the ranks to become the Camp Prefect.\nThe Praefectus Castrorum is the third-most senior commander of the Roman legion, after the Legate (Legatus) and the senior military tribune (Tribunus Laticlavius). He's responsible for looking after equipment and building works, but can command the legion when his superiors are absent.

Praefectus Cohortis

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Tribunus Cohortis, Tribunus Angusticlavius, Praefectus Alae (Prefect of the Wing), Praefectus Legionis, Praefectus Praetorio, Procurator Sexegenarius, Procurator Centenarius, Procurator Ducenarius, Tribunus Militi, Legatus, Legatus Legionis, Imperator Caesar · never on 17 of the cultures.

Effects: +1 Infantry Command

Praefectus Cohortis. Commander of an auxilary infantry unit or mixed unit with organic horsemen (Cohors Equitatae). Usually the first post in the equestrian military career (tres militiae), following assessment in a legion, and for some the last, it appears to have been of equal status and remuneration as the legionary centurionate. Men with good service records or influential friends could go on to a command a more prestigious cavalry unit or post of tribunus cohortis. A post of subpraefectus is known from the early principate, though its precise details are unclear.

Praefectus Legionis

Effects: +2 Cavalry Command, +1 Infantry Command

This man has reached the pinnacle of the Equestrian military career and secured a special promotion to command one of the legions. Under the early empire this was restricted to the command of one of the two legions defending Egypt and the essential grain supply, which was not to be entrusted to scheming Senators! Under the later empire, this was used more as Senators were slowly removed from legion command possibilities.

Praefectus Praetorio

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Praefectus Legionis, Procurator Sexegenarius, Procurator Centenarius, Procurator Ducenarius, Tribunus Militi, Legatus, Legatus Legionis, Imperator Caesar · never on 17 of the cultures.

Effects: +1 Cavalry Command, +2 Infantry Command

Originating as the commander of the Praetorian Guard, the Praetorian Prefect gradually acquired extensive legal and administrative functions, with its holders becoming the Emperor's chief aides.\nThe praetorians or imperial guards are commanded by one, two, or even three praefecti praetorio, who are chosen by the emperor from among the equites, and hold office at his pleasure. From the time of Alexander Severus, the post was open to senators as well, and if an equestrian was appointed he was at the same time raised to the senate. Down to the time of Constantine, who deprived the office of its military character, the prefecture of the guards was regularly held by tried soldiers, often by men who had fought their way up from the ranks. Eventually, the command seems to have been enlarged so as to include all the troops in Italy except the corps commanded by the city praefect (cohortes urbanae).

This man has succeeded in becoming a Praetor, a title granted by the Republic to men acting in one of two official capacities: The commander of an army, usually in the field, or the named commander before mustering the army; and an elected magistratus (magistrate) with various duties.

Preoccupied

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 4 levels · pulls against Focused, Determined · at most 40 characters may hold it.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Preoccupied1-1 InfluenceDistracted2-1 Management, -1 InfluenceDistant3-2 Management, -2 Influence, -1 CommandOblivious4-3 Management, -3 Command, -3 Influence
What the game says at each level

Preoccupied — This man always seems to have something else on his mind, while he still manages to handle his duties most of the time it is obvious that he would often rather be doing something else.

Distracted — His head might not be in the clouds but it’s certainly keeping the birds company. He still manages to drag himself back to earth often enough to keep the essentials in hand but many of his lesser responsibilities are usually forgotten.

Distant — It is obvious that this man has other interests and just doesn’t really care about fulfilling his duties. He sees such things as burdens and deals with them only when absolutely necessary.

Oblivious — His body is present, but his mind seems have left its proper domicile. He rarely even knows what’s going on around him, much less what he is supposed to do about it. He is both amusing and infuriating, but at least he seems to be happy.

Princeps Senatus

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Former Censor, Former Princeps Senatus · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

Effects: +2 Influence, +1 Law

This man has succeeded in becoming the Princeps Senatus (leader of the Senate).\nAlthough not officially a step in the cursus honorum, the Princeps Senatus is an extremely prestigious office, serving as the leader of the Senate and chosen to serve a five year term by each pair of Censors every five years. Censors can, however, confirm a Princeps Senatus for a period of another five years. He is chosen from all Patricians who have served as Consuls, with former Censors usually holding the office. The office originally granted the ability to speak first at session on the topic presented by the presiding magistrate, but eventually gained the power to open and close the senate sessions, decide the agenda, decide where the session should take place, impose order and other rules of the session, meet in the name of the senate with embassies of foreign countries, and write in the name of the senate letters and dispatches.

Proconsul

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Consul, Censor, Imperator Caesar · never on 21 of the cultures.

Effects: +2 Influence, +2 Management

Though not part of the Cursus Honorum, upon completing a term as either Praetor or Consul, he is required to serve a term as Propraetor and Proconsul, respectively, in one of Rome's many provinces. The Propraetors and Proconsuls have near autocratic authority within their selected province, and can only be vetoed by the current Consul or Praetor. Their abilities to govern are only limited by the decrees of the Senate or the people's assemblies, and the Tribune of the Plebs is unable to veto their acts as long as the governor remains at least a mile outside of Rome.

Proconsul Africae Proconsulari

What the game says at each level

Proconsul Africae Proconsulari — This man is the Proconsul of Africa Proconsularis, established in the most fertile part of what was formerly Carthaginian territory, after the Romans defeated Carthage in the Third Punic War. Utica was formed as the administrative capital, being awarded for its loyalty with a territorial increase stretching from Carthage to Hippo. Called the ‘granary of the empire’, North Africa, produced around one million tons of cereals each year, one-quarter of which was exported.

Proconsul Asiae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Asiae1no stat effectsProconsul Asiae2+1 InfluenceProconsul Asiae3+1 Trading, +1 Influence, +1 Law
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Asiae — This man is the Proconsul of Asia, created after Antiochos the Great had to give up Asia when the Romans crushed his army at the battle of Magnesia in 190 BC. After the Treaty of Apamea (188 BC), the entire territory was surrendered to Rome and placed under the control of a client king of Pergamon. With no apparent heir, Attalus III of Pergamon, having been a close ally, chose to bequeath his kingdom to Rome. Upon Attalus’s passing, in 133 BC, Manius Aquillius formally established the region as Asia province.

Proconsul Galliae Comatae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Galliae Comatae1no stat effectsProconsul Galliae Comatae2+1 Combat vs GallicProconsul Galliae Comatae3+3 Influence, +1 Combat vs Gallic
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Galliae Comatae — This man is the Proconsul of Gallia Comata (Latin: Long-haired Gaul), also called Tres Galliae, (Three Gauls), in Roman antiquity, the land of Gaul that included the three provinces of Aquitania, bordered by the Bay of Biscay on the west and the Pyrenees on the south; Celtica (or Gallia Lugdunensis), with Lugdunum (Lyon) as its capital, on the eastern border of Gaul and extending northwest to include Brittany; and Belgica (or Gallia Belgica), in the north, where Trier and Reims were the chief towns and many of the people were Germanic in origin. The only garrison in this province was a single cohort at Lugdunum to guard the imperial mint, but the Rhine army could and did move rapidly into the Gallia Comata if trouble occurred.

Proconsul Senatorius Achaeae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

Proconsul Senatorius Achaeae1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Achaeae2+2 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Achaeae3+2 Trading, +2 Law, +2 Influence
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Achaeae — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Achaea, controled by Rome since 146 BC when general Lucius Mummius sacked Corinth. There was peace until 88 BC, when Pontic king Mithridates VI began a campaign against Rome and obtained the support of many Greek City-States, but Lucius Cornelius Sulla crushed the rebellion and forced Mithridates out of Greece. Athens and Thebes were sacked and many artworks were destroyed or taken to Rome.\nAfter the defeat of Antony and Cleopatra, about 31 BC, Augustus separated Macedonia from Achaea, though it remained a Senatorial province, as under the Republic.\nThe land was devastated and remained so (with the exception of some larger cities) until Emperor Hadrian's restoration which triggered the Greek renaissance. He beautified Athens and restored many of the ruined Greek cities. The province remained prosperous and peaceful throughout Roman occupation. As it was a senatorial province, thus free from military men and legions, it was one of the most prestigious and sought-after provinces for senators to govern. Athens was the primary center of education for the imperial elite, rivaled only by Alexandria, and one of the most important cities in the Empire.

Proconsul Senatorius Africae Proconsulari

What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Africae Proconsulari — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Africa, a senatorial province after 27 BC, when the Republic had transformed into an Empire. During the reign of Augustus, the seat of provincial government was moved to a since rebuilt Carthage, although Utica did not lose its status as one of the foremost cities in the province. Nearly a century after the fall of Carthage, a new "Roman Carthage" was built on the same site by Julius Caesar between 49 and 44 BC. It soon became the center of the province. By the first century, Carthago had grown to be the second-largest city in the western Roman Empire, with a peak population of 500,000.

Proconsul Senatorius Asiae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Senatorius Asiae1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Asiae2+1 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Asiae3+1 Trading, +1 Influence, +1 Law
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Asiae — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Asia. The proconsul spends much of his year-long term traveling throughout the province hearing cases and conducting other judicial business at each of the assize centres. The beginning of the principate of Augustus signaled the rise of new cities in Mysia, Lydia and Phrygia, and saw an important change in the role of existing provincial cities, which evolved from autonomous city-states to Imperial administrative centres. The province became an elaborate system of self-governing cities, each responsible for its own economics, taxes, and law in its territory. The reign of Augustus further signaled the start of urbanization of Asia province, as public building became the defining characteristic of a city.

Proconsul Senatorius Baeticae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Senatorius Baeticae1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Baeticae2+2 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Baeticae3+2 Trading, +1 Law, +2 Influence, -3 Unrest
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Baeticae — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Baetica, an adjectival form of Baetis, the Roman name for the Guadalquivir River, whose fertile valley forms one of the most important parts of the province. In the reorganization of the Empire, in 14 BC, Hispania was remade into three Imperial provinces. \nFortune smiled on rich Baetica, also called Baetica Felix, and a dynamic, upwardly-mobile social and economic middling stratum developed there, which absorbed freed slaves and far outnumbered the rich elite.\nThe Senatorial province of Baetica became so secure that no Roman legion was required to be permanently stationed there. The south of the Iberian peninsula was agriculturally rich, providing exports of wine, olive oil and the fermented fish sauce called garum that were staples of the Mediterranean diet. Baetica was rich and utterly Romanized, so that the Emperor Vespasian granted the Ius latii that extended the rights pertaining to Roman citizenship (latinitas) to the inhabitants of Hispania, an honour that secured the loyalty of the Baetian elite and its middle class.

Proconsul Senatorius Bithyniae Pontoque

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Senatorius Bithyniae Pontoque1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Bithyniae Pontoque2+1 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Bithyniae Pontoque3+2 Trading, +2 Law, +1 Influence
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Bithyniae Pontoque — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Bithynia and Pontus.\nBithynia et Pontus was the name of a province of the Roman empire on the Black Sea coast of Anatolia. It was formed by the amalgamation of the former kingdoms of Bithynia (annexed by Rome 74 BC) and Pontus (annexed 64 BC). With the subjection of Pontus by Pompey in 64 BC little changed in the structuring of life, either for the oligarchies that controlled the cities or for the common people in cities or hinterland. The meaning of the name Pontus however underwent a change.\n\nPart of the kingdom was now annexed to the Roman Empire, being united with Bithynia in a double province called Pontus and Bithynia. After the reforms of Augustus, the province became senatorial. However, the eastern half of the old kingdom was administered as a client kingdom together with Colchis, its last king being Polemon II. (12 BC-74 AD). In AD 62 the country was constituted by Nero as a Roman province.

Proconsul Senatorius Ciliciae Cyproque

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Senatorius Ciliciae Cyproque1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Ciliciae Cyproque2+1 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Ciliciae Cyproque3+1 Influence, +1 Trading, +3 Unrest
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Ciliciae Cyproque — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Cilicia and Cyprus, reorganized by Julius Caesar in 47 BC, and in 27 BC by Augustus, who made further changes, reducing the province of Cilicia further. Cyprus was made a separate province; and Pamphylia with Isauria and Pisidia, after the death of Amyntas in 25 BC, was also made a separate province (the province of Galatia), to which Lycaonia was also attached. The result was that Cilicia was reduced to the original parts Campestris and Aspera, and renamed Syria-Cilicia Phoenice. From 22 BCE onwards, Cyprus was a senatorial province, divided into four districts centred around Paphus, Salamis, Amathus and Lapethus.

Proconsul Senatorius Corsicae Sardiniaeque

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Senatorius Corsicae Sardiniaeque1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Corsicae Sardiniaeque2+1 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Corsicae Sardiniaeque3+1 Farming, +1 Influence, +1 Law
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Corsicae Sardiniaeque — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Corsica and Sardinia.\nDuring the civil wars, as control of the islands became a strategic weapon in the hands of powerful generals, the wheat supply fed the armies of Sulla, Caesar and Octavian. Between 40 and 38 BC, Sextus Pompey captured Corsica and established strongholds on the island, from where he could threaten Italy with his pirate fleet, causing shortage of wheat supply in the capital. It was only after the battle of Naulochus in 36 BC, when Agrippa defeated Pompey’s fleet, that the islands were again under control of Augustus.\n\nThe Romans regarded both the islands and their people as backward and unhealthy, in all likelihood due to the long-standing presence of malaria. Strabo mentioned that the populations residing in the mountains were still not completely pacified during his time and eventually resorted to live off plunder, pillaging other Sardinian communities and sailing with their ships to raid the Etrurian shores.\nCorsica and Sardinia were kept in a scarcely urbanised state and came mostly to be used as places of exile. While neglected, the islands still provided Rome with much of the grain supply, wax, great number of sailors for her military fleets, while Sardinia was also one of the main suppliers of metal.

Proconsul Senatorius Corsicae Sardiniaeque — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Corsica and Sardinia.\nDuring the civil wars, as control of the islands became a strategic weapon in the hands of powerful generals, the wheat supply fed the armies of Sulla, Caesar and Octavian. Between 40 and 38 BC, Sextus Pompey captured Corsica and established strongholds on the island, from where he could threaten Italy with his pirate fleet, causing shortage of wheat supply in the capital. It was only after the battle of Naulochus in 36 BC, when Agrippa defeated Pompey’s fleet, that the islands were again under control of Augustus.\n\nThe Romans regarded both the islands and their people as backward and unhealthy, in all likelihood due to the long-standing presence of malaria. Strabo mentioned that the populations residing in the mountains were still not completely pacified during his time and eventually resorted to live off plunder, pillaging other Sardinian communities and sailing with their ships to raid the Etrurian shores.\nCorsica and Sardinia were kept in a scarcely urbanised state and came mostly to be used as places of exile. While neglected, the islands still provided Rome with much of the grain supply, wax, great number of sailors for her military fleets, while Sardinia was also one of the main suppliers of metal.

Proconsul Senatorius Cretae Cyrenaicaeque

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

Proconsul Senatorius Cretae Cyrenaicaeque1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Cretae Cyrenaicaeque2+1 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Cretae Cyrenaicaeque3+1 Trading, +1 Law, +1 Influence
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Cretae Cyrenaicaeque — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Crete and Cyrenaica, a senatorial province of the Roman empire created in 20 BC. It comprises the island of Crete and the region of Cyrenaica in North Africa.\nPtolemy Apion, the last king of the Hellenistic Kingdom of Cyrenaica left his kingdom to the Roman Republic when he died childless in 96 BC. Rome readily accepted this inheritance from Ptolemy Apion but preferred to leave the administration to local rulers, rather than enforcing direct control. However, by the 70s BC, civil uprisings by Jewish settlers began to destabilise the province and the Senate was forced to take action.\nMarcus Antonius Creticus attacked Crete in 71 BC and was repelled. Rome sent Quintus Caecilius Metellus with three legions to the island and after a ferocious three-year campaign, Crete was conquered, with Metellus earning the agnomen "Creticus" for his efforts. Gortyn seems to have been pro-Roman and was rewarded by being made the capital of the joint province of Creta et Cyrenaica.

Proconsul Senatorius Galliae Narbonensi

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Senatorius Galliae Narbonensi1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Galliae Narbonensi2+1 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Galliae Narbonensi3+1 Trading, +1 Influence, +1 Law
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Galliae Narbonensi — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Gallia Narbonensis, from where Julius Caesar began his Gallic Wars. Caesar rebuilt Narbonne (the provincial capital) and built the cities of Forum Julium and Arles, while also granting citizenship to many communities in the province. In 49 BCE, the city of Massalia sided with the Pompeians during the civil war. After the war ended, Massalia lost all of its independence and was fully subject to Roman rule. After becoming Emperor, Augustus made Gallia Narbonensis a senatorial province governed by a Proconsul.\nBordering directly on Italy, control of the province gives the Roman state several advantages such as control of the land route between Italy and the Iberian peninsula and control of the lucrative trade routes of the Rhone valley through which flow commercial goods between Gaul and the trading centre of Massalia.

Proconsul Senatorius Macedoniae Epiroque

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Senatorius Macedoniae Epiroque1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Macedoniae Epiroque2+2 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Macedoniae Epiroque3+2 Trading, +2 Influence, +2 Law
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Macedoniae Epiroque — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Macedonia, one of the main arenas of the civil wars at the end of the Roman Republic. After Pompey left Italy at the onset of the Civil War, the fleeing senators set themselves up in Thessalonica as a senate in exile, until Caesar defeated Pompey at Pharsalus. In 42 BC, the Second Triumvirate defeated the forces of Brutus and Cassius at the battle of Philippi. Then, after the battle of Actium in 31 BC, Macedonia ultimatelly came under Octavian’s control.\nAugustus later split Greece (which became the province Achaea) from Macedonia. The establishment of new provinces to the north and the consolidation of Roman control in the Balkans in general led to a decline in the military importance of Macedonia to Rome, as the legions defending the northern border were henceforth based in Dalmatia, Moesia, and Thrace. Nevertheless, the province continued to provide a vital role in the transport of supplies from Italy to the northern and eastern borders of the Empire, as well as serving as a source of manpower.\n\nThe reign of Augustus began a long period of peace, prosperity and wealth for Macedonia, although its importance in the economic standing of the Roman world diminished when compared to its neighbor Asia Minor.

Proconsul Senatorius Siciliae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Proconsul Senatorius Siciliae1no stat effectsProconsul Senatorius Siciliae2+1 InfluenceProconsul Senatorius Siciliae3+1 Farming, +1 Influence, -2 Unrest
What the game says at each level

Proconsul Senatorius Siciliae — This man is the Proconsul of the senatorial province of Sicily.\nAfter the death of Julius Caesar, Sicily soon fell into the hands of Sextus Pompey, who began collecting proscribed individuals and slaves and carrying out various acts of piracy. It was only after the battle of Naulochus in 36 BC that his pirate navy was defeated by Agrippa, after which Sicily was harshly punished by Augustus and left devastated. The island became an imperial property, with Augustus settling his veterans in newly established coloniae -- at the end of his reorganisation of the empire, six Sicilian cities had become coloniae: Syracuse, Tauromenium, Panormus, Catania, Tyndaris, and Thermae Himerenses, while Messina, Lipara, and perhaps Lilybaion, Agregentum, and Halaesa were made into municipiae – a status significantly lower than that of colonia.\n\nThe influx of population represented by these foundations may have been intended to compensate for a demographic slump resulting from the war with Sextus Pompey, or from Augustus' excorciation of the island after his victory, but in any case the influx of Italian veterans played a decisive role in the diffusion of Latin language in Sicily.\nFiscally, the grain tithe (decuma) was replaced by the stipendium, a property tax, and there may also have been a poll tax introduced.

Procrastinator

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Focused, Determined, Dedicated Trainer · at most 1 character may hold it.

Effects: -1 Management, -1 Command, -2 Movement Points · grants the epithet the Delayer

Famous for his inability to make a decision, this man spends hours, if not days, deliberating over the smallest details, often leading to missed opportunities and frustrated officers.

Procurator Augusti Cappadociae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Procurator Augusti Cappadociae1no stat effectsProcurator Augusti Cappadociae2+1 Influence, +2 Combat vs CaucasianProcurator Augusti Cappadociae3-2 Unrest, +2 Law, +1 Influence, +2 Combat vs Caucasian
What the game says at each level

Procurator Augusti Cappadociae — This man is the Imperial Procurator of Cappadocia, a province of the Roman empire in Anatolia with its capital at Caesarea (Mazaka, today Kayseri). The Cappadocians, supported by Rome against Mithridates VI of Pontus, elected a native lord, Ariobarzanes, to become king (93 BC). His dynasty came to an end when a certain Archelaus was installed in its stead by favour first of Antony and then of Octavian and maintained tributary relations until AD 17 when emperor Tiberius, on Archelaus' death in disgrace, reduced Cappadocia at last to a Roman province.\nUpon annexation, the province was governed by a governor of Equestrian rank with the title Procurator. The Procurators commanded only auxiliary military units and looked to the Senatorial ranked Imperial Legate of Syria for direction.\nFollowing the Roman civil war of 69, the Emperor Vespasian upgraded the province to Senatorial rank, making its governor equal in rank with that of Syria. As a Senatorial province during the middle of the second century AD, Cappadocia retained a permanent military garrison of three legions and several auxiliary units, totally over 28,000 troops. The military presence in Cappadocia served as an important response force against invasions from the Parthian Empire and allowed the Roman's easy intervention into the affairs of their client kingdom of Armenia.

Procurator Augusti Regno Bosporo

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Procurator Augusti Regno Bosporo1no stat effectsProcurator Augusti Regno Bosporo2+2 InfluenceProcurator Augusti Regno Bosporo3+2 Law, +2 Influence, -2 Unrest
What the game says at each level

Procurator Augusti Regno Bosporo — This man is the Imperial Procurator of the Kingdom of Bosporos.\nMithridates VI. entrusted the Bosporus Cimmerius to his son Machares who however deserted to the Romans. Even when driven out of his own kingdom by Pompey, Mithridates VI. was strong enough to regain the Cimmerian Bosporus and Machares slew himself. Subsequently the Bosporans again rose in revolt under Pharnaces II. After the death of Mithridates VI. (63 BC), Pharnaces II. (63-47 BC) made his submission to Pompey, then tried to regain his dominion during the civil war, but was defeated by Roman Dictator Gaius Julius Caesar at Zela and was later killed by his former governor and son-in-law Asander. \nThe Bosporan Kingdom of Aspurgus was a "Client State" of the Roman Empire, helped by Roman garrisons. Aspurgus (8 BC-38 AD) founded a line of kings which endured with certain interruptions until 341. Aspurgus adopted the Roman name Tiberius Julius because he received Roman citizenship and enjoyed the patronage of the first two Roman Emperors, Augustus and Tiberius.\nThey carried on a perpetual war with the native tribes and in this were supported by their Roman suzerains who even lent the assistance of a garrison and a fleet. Control of this area brings with it the very real possibility of war with the Sarmatians, so it may be difficult to hold.

Procurator Augusti Thraciae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Procurator Augusti Thraciae1no stat effectsProcurator Augusti Thraciae2+1 InfluenceProcurator Augusti Thraciae3+10 Tax Collection, +1 Influence, +2 Law
What the game says at each level

Procurator Augusti Thraciae — This man is the Imperial Procurator of Thracia.\nThe Odrysian kingdom of Thrace was ruled independentally by the native hellenized kings until the Roman civil war when they sided with Pompey. After the latter's defeat, Thrace was at least nominally a client state of Rome. The Odrysian kingdom of Thrace became a Roman client kingdom c. 20 BC, while the Greek city-states on the Black Sea coast came under Roman control as civitates foederatae ("allied" cities with internal autonomy). After the death of the Thracian king Rhoemetalces III in 46 AD and an unsuccessful anti-Roman revolt, the kingdom was annexed as the Roman province of Thracia.\n\nThe new province encompassed not only the lands of the former Odrysian realm, but also the north-eastern portion of the province of Macedonia as well as the islands of Thasos, Samothrace and Imbros in the Aegean Sea. To the north, Thracia bordered the province of Moesia; initially, the provincial boundary ran at a line north of the Haeumus Mountains, including the cities of Nicopolis ad Istrum and Marcianopolis in Thracia, but by the end of the 2nd century AD the border had moved south along the Haemus.\nThe province's first capital, where the Roman governor resided, was Heraclea Perinthus. \nThracia was an imperial province, headed initially by a procurator, and after 107/109 by a legatus Augusti pro praetore. Otherwise, the internal structure of the old Thracian kingdom was retained and only gradually superseded by Roman institutions.\nAs it was an interior province, far from the borders of the Empire, and having a major Roman road (Via Egnatia) that passed through the region, Thrace remained peaceful and prosperous until the Crisis of the Third Century.

Procurator Centenarius

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Procurator Ducenarius, Senator, Tribunus Militi, Legatus, Legatus Legionis, Imperator Caesar · never on 21 of the cultures.

Effects: +2 Influence, +1 Management

The word 'procurator' is derived from the Latin verb procurare, which means 'to take care'. When the Emperor Augustus had to organize the empire, he used procurators to manage his extensive private and public domains. He had to. In the provinces that were directly under his control, he could not employ quaestors, because no senator would think of serving under someone who was technically his equal. Therefore, he made procurators responsible for the taxation. Other procurators administered Augustus' private finances and his possessions in and near Rome and in the senatorial provinces. The procurators serving in the senatorial provinces increasingly received juridical powers, a practice that was made lawful in 53 by the Emperor Claudius. During his reign, we also encounter the first procurators with the full powers of a provincial governor. For example, when Judaea was annexed in 41, a procurator was appointed as its ruler, second only to the emperor. There Were 49 known positions of this type, and they were paid 100000 denarii (Centenarius) a year.

Cultures that never receive it (21)

Gallic · Germanic · Dacian · Celtiberian · Brittonic · Thracian · Greek · Caucasian · Anatolian · Iranian · Arab · Indian · Egyptian · Ethiopian · Phoenician · Libyan · Scythian · Western Hellenistic · Eastern Hellenistic · Iberian · Illyrian

How it is gained — ending a turn in a settlement
WhenPointsChanceConditions
ending a turn in a settlement+2100%of Roman culture · has Equestrian · does not have Senator · has Procurator Sexegenarius · does not have Procurator Centenarius · does not have Procurator Ducenarius (plus hidden conditions)

Procurator Iudaeae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Procurator Iudaeae1no stat effectsProcurator Iudaeae2+2 Influence, +40 Bribe ResistanceProcurator Iudaeae3+2 Influence, +3 Unrest, +40 Bribe Resistance
What the game says at each level

Procurator Iudaeae — This man is the Procurator of Iudaea, established after the Third Mithridatic War, with Pompey's restructuring of the Hellenistic kingdoms in the East. Pompey remained there to secure the area and subsequently Judaea became a client state of Rome. The region at the time was not a peaceful place. Queen Salome Alexandra had recently died and her sons Hyrcanus II and Aristobulus II fought against each other in a civil war. In 63 BC Aristobulus was besieged in Jerusalem by his brother's armies. He sent an envoy to Marcus Aemilius Scaurus, Pompey's representative in the area. Aristobulus offered a massive bribe to be rescued, which Pompey promptly accepted. Afterwards Aristobulus accused Scaurus of extortion. Since Scaurus was Pompey's brother in law and protégée, the general retaliated by putting Hyrcanus in charge of the kingdom as Prince and High Priest. When Pompey was defeated by Julius Caesar, Hyrcanus was succeeded by his courtier Antipater the Idumaean also known as Antipas as the first Roman Procurator.\n\nBetween 57-55 BC Aulus Gabinius, proconsul of Syria, split the former Hasmonean Kingdom of Israel into five districts of Sanhedrin/Synedrion (councils of law). Both Caesar and Antipater were killed in 44 BC and the Idumean Herod the Great, Antipater's son, was designated "King of the Jews" by the Roman Senate in 40 BC, but didn't gain military control until 37 BC. During his reign the last representatives of the Maccabees were eliminated and the great port of Caesarea Maritima was built. He died in 4 BC and his kingdom was divided among his sons who became tetrarchs ("rulers of a quarter part").

Promised Lands

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · at most 2 characters may hold it · never on 17 of the cultures.

Effects: -10 Tax Collection, -2 Influence, +1 Training Units, +1 Troop Morale, +1 Farming

This general has promised his troops tax-free land as a reward for their loyalty and service. While his soldiers are dreaming of green fields and fertile farms, the other nobles complain about such populist move, knowing that armies bound by such oaths may prove more loyal to their commander than to the state itself.

Promising Commander

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 5 levels · pulls against Inferior Commander.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Promising Commander2+1 CommandGood Commander4+2 CommandSuperior Commander10+3 Command, +10 Siege EngineeringGreat Commander16+4 Command, +20 Siege Engineering
Legendary Commander30+5 Command, +30 Siege Engineering
What the game says at each level

Promising Commander — This man shows the early spark of a competent military commander, gradually improving his skills and growing more confident in his role.

Good Commander — Victory after victory, this man has proven himself a competent military leader on the battlefield.

Superior Commander — This man has developed a talent for command, making him the sort of leader soldiers are proud to follow. Where his standard rises, victory soon follows.

Great Commander — This man has an almost unsurpassed ability to command in battle and this confidence inspires his troops. His enemies pray to their gods when they hear he marches against them.

Legendary Commander — His proven ability to command in battle has made this man a legend among his troops, who see him as a giant among men. Future generations will invoke his name before battle as one calls upon the gods.

Retinue whose arrival is tied to this trait: Asclepiodotus the Philosopher, Celtic Ally, Scout.

Promising Raider

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · pulls against Poor Raider.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Promising Raider2+5 LootingAccomplished Raider5+10 Looting, +1 Attack, -1 LawGreat Raider10+15 Looting, +1 Attack, +1 Troop Morale, -5 Trading, +2 Bodyguard Valour, -2 Law, +1 Influence
What the game says at each level

Promising Raider — This man has shown some promise as a raider, though how truly skilled he is at this art remains unclear...

Accomplished Raider — This man is an accomplished raider, and understands the implicit 'theory' and mechanisms behind raiding and acquiring booty from his enemies. While no legend at the art, he is well-respected amongst his peers and especially his men for his aptitude and experience in this area.

Great Raider — This man's name is synonymous with raiding, pillaging and sacking, and the men who join him on his raids have total faith in him. He has total command of the mechanisms behind a successful raid, and is a true legend in the art of raiding. Inexperienced leaders of raiding bands and fellow raid leaders alike follow his fine example... Or are jealous of his immense success. Unfortunately, this penchant for raiding has come at the cost of trade, as he does not agree with trading with other peoples. Instead, he believes in raiding them!

Propraetor

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Praetor, Consul, Imperator Caesar · never on 21 of the cultures.

Effects: +1 Influence, +1 Law, +1 Command

Though not part of the Cursus Honorum, upon completing a term as either Praetor or Consul, this man is required to serve a term as Propraetor and Proconsul in one of Rome's many provinces. He has near autocratic authority within the assigned province, and can only be vetoed by the current Consul or Praetor. His abilities to govern are only limited by the decrees of the Senate or the people's assemblies, and the Tribune of the Plebs is unable to veto his acts as long as he remains at least a mile outside of Rome.

Cultures that never receive it (21)

Gallic · Germanic · Dacian · Celtiberian · Brittonic · Thracian · Greek · Caucasian · Anatolian · Iranian · Arab · Indian · Egyptian · Ethiopian · Phoenician · Libyan · Scythian · Western Hellenistic · Eastern Hellenistic · Iberian · Illyrian

How it is gained — at the start of the character's turn
WhenPointsChanceConditionsWhenPointsChanceConditions
at the start of the character's turn+2100%has Praetor · does not have Imperator Caesar (plus hidden conditions)at the start of the character's turn+2100%is a general (plus hidden conditions)

Propraetor Bithyniae

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

LevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffectsLevelAt pointsEffects
Propraetor Bithyniae1no stat effectsPropraetor Bithyniae2+2 InfluencePropraetor Bithyniae3+2 Trading, +2 Influence
What the game says at each level

Propraetor Bithyniae — This man is the Propraetor of Bithynia. In 74 BC, King Nicomedes died and, hoping to secure his kingdom from further Pontic aggression, bequeathed his kingdom to Rome. The Senate immediately voted to annex the kingdom as a province directly governed by the Republic, but it was only after 64 BC, as Pompey returned from the conclusion of the Third Mithridatic War, that Rome officially annexed Bithynia. As a Roman province, the boundaries of Bithynia frequently varied and for administrative purposes, it was commonly united with the province of Pontus.

Propraetor Ciliciae Cyproque — This man is the Propraetor of Cilicia and Cyprus. Cilicia Trachea became the haunt of pirates who were only subdued by Pompey in 67 BC following the Battle of Korakesion, and Tarsus was made the capital of the Roman province of Cilicia. Cilicia Pedias became Roman territory in 103 BC, first conquered by Marcus Antonius Orator in his campaign against pirates, with Sulla acting as its first governor, foiling an invasion of Mithridates.

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · 3 levels · at most 1 character may hold it · never on 21 of the cultures.

What the game says at each level

Propraetor Galliae Cisalpinae — This man is the Propraetor of Cisalpine Gaul (meaning Gaul on this side of the Alps), a geographical area in the territory of northern Italy mainly inhabited by Celts. Sometimes referred to as Gallia Citerior ("Hither Gaul"), Provincia Ariminum, or Gallia Togata ("Toga-wearing Gaul", indicating the region's early Romanization). Gallia Transpadana denoted that part of Cisalpine Gaul between the Padus (now the Po River) and the Alps, while Gallia Cispadana was the part to the south of the river.

Proscripted

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders.

Effects: -5 Influence, -5 Command, -5 Management

This man had been proscribed by his enemies. His name was published in public lists and not only was he stripped of citizenship, he was also marked as an outlaw, to be killed by any citizen with total legal immunity. His land, money, and houses were confiscated and sold at public auctions. His sons and grandsons shall lose citizenship rights and are banned from holding public office.

What the game says at each level

Prudent Commander — Known for his cautiousness and slow marches while in campaign, this man prefers a war of maneuver and attrition against his enemies, rather than risky pitched battles.

Extremely Cautious Commander — This general avoids unnecessary engagements, planning every move to the last detail. Why fight a battle when you can simply wait out the enemy’s supplies?

Overly Cautious Commander — This general has gained a reputation for almost never making a move unless victory is absolutely certain, which tends to work but frustrates many who prefer to bring the war to the enemy.

Following the recent loss of a great number of Carthaginian citizens in battle, the city of Carthage has declared a period of public mourning to honour their memory. Now, the city walls are draped with black fabrics, a reminder of the lives lost. Citizens walk around in silence, and the marketplace feels quieter than ever. Those brave enough to laugh in public find themselves silenced by the stares of fellow citizens paying their respects.

What the game says at each level

Publicani - Minority Share Tax Farmer — Being a member of the Equestrian Order gives this man the right to collect taxes for the Roman state. The expense of buying a license for this has driven him to buy a minority stake in a societas (company). Although small, it will no doubt be very lucrative. Perhaps too soon for magisterial ambitions. Publicani are known primarily for their role as state contracted tax collectors. However, although this ended up being their primary function they often supply the army and supervise public building projects.

Publicani - Medium Share Tax Farmer — This man has been able to acquire a larger share of the societas, meaning more revenue. With the increased wealth he may be able to run for a magisterial office.

Publicani - Majority Share Tax Farmer — Owning a very large share of the societas he is involved in, has amassed great wealth for this man. Although generally reviled by the majority of the population, he can easily afford to run for office and forge a path into the Senate.

Punisher Judge — In this man's opinion true justice is about proper punishment of all the guilty.

Severe — Justice and mercy are mutually exclusive in this man's eyes, regardless of the facts. Before stepping into his court, people have started making sacrifices in the temples not to appease the gods, but so that the gods may appease this man.

Merciless Judge — Anyone hoping for leniency from this man would have better luck pleading their case to a rock. In fact, the only people happy to see him are the executioners, who he keeps in regular work.

Retinue whose arrival is tied to this trait: Erasistratus, Pet Lion, Zurvanist Priest.

Punisher of Capua

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders.

Effects: +1 Influence, +10 Looting

Capua made its choice when it opened its gates to our enemies, and now it paid the price. Its ruling class has been executed, its citizens enslaved, its land stripped and handed to Roman colonists. Rome does not forget betrayal, and it does not forgive it. Let every allied city remember what happened here.

This man is the fruit of a union between the most influential pro-Punic Numidian families and the Carthaginian aristocracy. His knowledge of Numidian traditions will be invaluable when calling upon their help. Educated in Carthage, he stands poised for a high-ranking position in Carthage’s political arena. Puno-Nuraghic

Appears on every character, agents included · pulls against Carthaginian Aristocrat, Carthaginian Citizen, Puno-Numidian, Cannanite, Puno-Iberian, Puno-Libyan, Puno-Sikel, 'Amm Gades, 'Amm Iboshim, Liby-Phoenician, Puno-Rasenna, Puno-Campanian, Puno-Bruttian.

Effects: no stat effects

Though the chiefs of the Nuraghic towns and cities are not elevated to high positions in the Carthaginian administration, this man is the child of a rare union between Nuraghic chiefs and Carthaginian citizens. Raised, at least for a time, among the vast forests and hills of Sardinia, he is at home in rugged landscapes, and all too familiar with the methods of warfare. Although he holds Carthaginian beliefs, his roots make him more comfortable in the company of soldiers and scouts than among senators.

Pylaimenian

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · sons inherit it 1% of the time · never on 9 of the cultures. Once it reaches Pylaimenian it cannot drop back.

Effects: +1 Influence, +1 Fertility

This man is a descendant of the proud king Pylaimenes of the Paphlagonian Eneti, who bravely fought under the walls of sacred Troy. Pylaimenes gave his life in honourable battle with Menelaos of Sparta, while his son Harpalion was killed by the wicked Cretan Meriones. These sacrifices have not been forgotten, and the royal house of Pylaimenes has ruled Paphlagonia until the present day, taking great pride in their past.

Cultures that never receive it (9)

Gallic · Germanic · Dacian · Celtiberian · Brittonic · Thracian · Phoenician · Libyan · Roman

Granted by events, at birth or by inheritance.

Pythionikes

Appears on family members — generals, governors, leaders · pulls against Winter · at most 5 characters may hold it · never on 17 of the cultures.

Effects: -1 Unrest, +1 Influence

Pythiois Nikesas!\nThis man has brought great honor and fame to himself and his people. It is not a common occurrence to stand before the crowds in Delphi and be crowned with the wreath of bay laurel, but he has done it, and won a great victory in the Pythionikes! The Pythian Games may be second only to the Olympics, but you’d never know it from the way he talks about his win.
